; =======================================================================

; Here are some base addresses of hardware items that arise
;  (or don't arise) in the bootrom code
;
;  0xff440000 - CRU (clock and reset unit)
;  0xff100000 - GRF (general register file)
;  0xff0d0000 - SGRF (secure mode general register file)
;  0xff060000 - Crypto
;  0xff140000 - PMU (power management unit)
;  0xff500000 - SDMMC
;  0xff520000 - eMMC
;  0xff580000 - USB2_OTG
;  0xff230000 - GPIO2
;  0xff260000 - Efuse non secure access
;  0xff0b0000 - Efuse secure access
;  0xff090000 - Internal SRAM (36K?)
;  0xff0a0000 - OTP_S
;  0xff190000 - SPI
;
;
;  ff000000 - I2S0 - never seen
;  ff010000 - I2S1 - never seen
;  ff020000 - I2S2 - never seen

; =======================================================================

; So what about aarch64, this is a poor place for it, but I won't
; let that stop me.  Here is a brief tutorial.
; There is actually no better way to learn a processor instruction
;  set other than studying disassembled code like this.
; You certainly can learn by writing code, but until you gain a
;  lot of experience you are likely to only use a part of the
;  instruction set.
; And this is a great place given that it is closely
; followed by a lot of example code to see the instruction
; set in use!
;
; We have 31 general registers x0 to x30
; with "x" in the name, we are talking about a 64 bit register
; with "w" in the name, we are talking about a 32 bit register
; Some are special (as per compiler use) --
;  x29 is the FP (frame pointer)
;  x30 is the LR (link register)
; We also have xzr, wzr, which is a permanently zero register when read.
; We have the sp, but it is not associated with a general register.
; We also have the pc, also not available as a general register.
;
; instructions "flow" from right to left, as:
; name dest, src1, src2
; name dest, src1, immediate
;
; ldr dest, [src] -- load from address in src register
; str src, [dest] -- store to address in src register
; stp src1, src2, [dest] -- store a pair of registers
; strh, ldrh -- store and load half words
; strb, ldrb -- store and load bytes
; 
; So, what happens when you load a small thing into a 64 bit register.
;  as near as I can tell you get zeros filling the rest of the register.
;  if the instruction ends with an "s" you get sign extension
;
; It is worth noting that aarch64 has nothing like the stm/ldm
;  multiple register load and store instructions
;
; cbz, cbnz -- compare register to zero and branch
; tbz, tbnz -- test bit and branch if zero
;
; Note that "ldr" instructions load a 64 bit value from memory,
; typically from an address given as an offset from the location
; of the current instruction, but we would never know that.
;
; What we do need to pay attention to on 64 bit loads is how the
; values are displayed.  The ARM runs little endian in our case,
; so our memory dump shows the low 32 bit value (swapped to big endian)
; first, followed by the high 32 bit value (also swapped big endian).
;
; I wrote a little program to dump areas with 64 bit constants
; and I think patched this into the relevant areas, so this is
; no longer a source of confusion reading this listing.
;
; adrp is an interesting instruction.  The idea is to load
; a 64 bit constand into a register within a single 32 bit
; instruction.  The disassembly shows the final value that
; will be placed into the register, and the trickery can be
; viewed as a black box.  However, you get the base address
; of a 4K page, so you will need to add an offset.
; A pair of instructions such as this are often used:
;		adrp	x0, 0xff8c0000
;		add	x1, x0, #0x18
; Another scheme is to use adrp to load the page base address
; as the following, then use an offset in the addressing
; mode of a subsequent instruction, like this:
;
;		adrp	x1, 0xffff4000
;		ldr	w1, [x1, #3692]
;
; Early code in the bootrom eschews using the adrp instruction scheme
; and relies on a block of 64 bit constants that follow the code.
; This early code was probably written in assembler, and the bulk
; of the code was generated by a compiler which used the adrp scheme.
;
; Note also the annoying business of the assembler in objdump using
; decimal base offsets, I would much prefer these as hex, and intend
; to write a post-processor to make them so.
;
; ARM is as bad as the m68k for addressing modes.
; to start with, what is the difference here:
;		ldr	w3, [x0, #4]
;		ldr	w3, [x0], #4
;		ldr	w3, [x1, #4]!
;
; The first is easy, we add 4 to the value in x0 and fetch from that location.
; The second is called "post indexing".  We fetch from the address in x0,
; then after the fetch, add 4 to x0.
; The third is called "pre indexing".  We add 4 to x1 first, then use
; that changed value to fetch from memory
;
; bl and ret handle subroutines
; bl is "branch and link" and places the return in lr (x30)
; ret returns by copying x30 into the pc
; note that the stack is not involved at all,
;  a subroutine which intends to call other subroutines will
;  need to free up lr by pushing it onto the stack and restoring it
;  a leaf subroutine won't need to do this at all.
; 
; br reg -- branches to address in reg
;   this is just what we need to run code we just loaded
;
; csel is "condition select"
;   if the condition is true, the first register gets copied to the destination
;   otherwise the second register gets copied.
;   This requires a previous "cmp" instruction or some such to set the condition
;   Here is an example:
;       csel    x20, x1, x0, eq
; cset
;  This sets a register to 1 if a condition is true, else it sets 0
;  this will need to follow a cmp or some other instruction that sets
;  condition codes.  I see it used in 2 places in this code as follows.
;  The following test asks if register w19 is non-zero
;  The following "and" seems like belt and suspenders
;  It seems to me we could just specify x0 in the cset instruction
;
;	cmp	w19, #0x0
;	cset	w0, ne
;	and	x0, x0, #0x1
;
; -----------
; Here are some notes on the stack and subroutine conventions
;  apparently the sp needs to stay 16 bit aligned, so you will
;  always see a multiple of 16 bytes being subtracted on
; subroutine entry.
; Now consider this:
;	sub	sp, sp, #0x20
;	stp	x29, x30, [sp, #16]
;	add	x29, sp, #0x10
; Here we allocate 16 bytes more than the bare minimum
; We dump x29 (FP) and x30 (LR) at the bottom of this area
; remember these are 64 bit registers, so this takes 16 bytes
; then we set up x29 (the FP) to point to where these two registers
; were saved.  The sp thus points to a block of memory above
; these two registers which can be used for anything local.
; The case where we sub 0x10 is a degenerate case where no
; local memory is being allocated.
;
; This rigamarole suggests that code like this was generated by a
; compiler, but this is by no means certain.
;
; -----------
; Condition codes
; like any good processor, the ARM 64 has these.
;    and it has 4 bits (N,Z,C,V)
; The ARM 32 allowed almost any instruction to be conditionally executed
;  based on the CC, but ARM 64 has done away with that, which I think
;  is a good thing.
; Testing the CC is done by branch, select, and compare instructions
; We have mnemonics for certain CC combinations, as follows
;
;  EQ - Z set
;  NE - Z clear
;  CS - C set (unsigned ge)
;  CC - C clear (unsigned lt)
;  MI - N set (negative)
;  PL - N clear (zero or positive)
;  VS - V set (overflow)
;  VC - V clear
;  HI - C set,Z clear (unsigned gt)
;  LS - not (C set and Z clear) (unsigned le)
;  GE - N, V the same (signed ge)
;  LT - N, V different (signed lt)
;  GT - Z clear, N,V the same (signed gt)
;  LE - not (Z clear, N,V the same) (signed le)
;  AL - always (unconditional branch) - any CC
;    (in this case, we just have "b"
;
; Note that the notations of GE, LT in the above are completely useless
; without some convention of what is being compared to what.
; The way to think about it is as the result of a preceding "cmp"
; where we compare "cmp A, B", in this case GE refers to A >= B
;
; Note that I never see vs or vc (in this code anyway)
; The condition codes are used by b, cset, csel (others?).
;
; I always get annoyed at this whole condition code business.
;  so I am going to go on at some length in the following.
; There are endless opportunities for double think depending
;  on what order the operands are compared and whether you
;  are talking about signed or unsigned values.
;
; The way to get your mind around these is to realize that you
;  really need to think about multi-instruction "idioms".
; If you think about C programming, you never deal with condition codes,
;  you just compare numbers, so what are the idioms for comparing numbers?
; A conditional branch will almost invariably be preceeded by a
;  cmp instruction like the following:

;	ffff0fdc:	71000b9f 	cmp	w28, #0x2
;	ffff0fe0:	54fffb83 	b.cc	0xffff0f50  // b.lo, b.ul, b.last

; The above says to branch if w28 < 2  (if we have unsigned values)

;	ffff0298:	6b3342df 	cmp	w22, w19, uxtw
;	ffff029c:	54fffec3 	b.cc	0xffff0274  // b.lo, b.ul, b.last

; And the above will branch if w22 < w19 (given unsigned values)
;  the uxtw notation (to do an unsigned extend) comfirms the unsigned context

; It is vital to know whether the values are intended to be treated as signed or unsigned
; It is also vital to consider the order of the two items.

; Let's talk about  "cmp reg, #val", which is a common idiom.
; In this case (thinking unsigned) b.cs is a branch if reg >= val
; In the other case (thinking unsigned) b.cc is a branch if reg < val

; A note on the uxtw notation in the above is in order.
; We can have uxt[b,h,w].
; These indicate to zero extend a byte, halfword (16 bits), or word (32 bits)
;  from the source element
;
; I also see "uxtx" in a handful of adds, as in the following
;  add     x6, x0, x1, uxtx #4
; These are all already 64 bit registers, so I don't see the point,
;  but I am going to pass this by for now.
;
; ubfx is a "bitfield extract" instruction
;	ubfx	w1, w0, #6, #4
;  the above extracts from w0 to w1
;  6 is the starting bit, 4 is the number of bits
;  this is a nice way to replace the usual mask and shift
;  In C code, this would be:
;	dest = (src >> lsb) & ((1 << width) - 1);

;	lsr	w0, w0, #0
;  the above raises an interesting issue, this particular case is a noop.
;  but consider the following
;	lsr	w0, w7, #0
;  this just moves w7 to w0.  Why not just use a mov instruction?
;  some sources say that only values of 1-31 are allowed.
;  I wrote some test code and convinced myself by experiment that
;  this just copies registers.
;  It is odd that this is used in several places.
;
; tst does a bitwise AND of two operands, discarding the result

; ==========================================================================
; ==========================================================================

; All cores start here (address ffff0000)
;
; We first read a register in the SGRF at offset ff0d0000
; The PMU is at ff140000
;  the PMU regs are described on pp. 253 .. in the TRM
;  as near as I can tell the SGRF are registers that may
;  be manipulated in secure mode, and I have yet to find documentation

ffff0000 <.text>:
ffff0000:	58000940 	ldr	x0, 0xffff0128		; ff0d0000 (SGRF)
ffff0004:	b9400000 	ldr	w0, [x0]
ffff0008:	367800a0 	tbz	w0, #15, 0xffff001c	; branch if SGRF[0] bit 15 is zero

; We are here if the mystery register bit is non-zero
; Seens it checks for reset cause
ffff000c:	580008a0 	ldr	x0, 0xffff0120		; ff440094 (CRU, CRU_GLB_RST_ST register TRM p.44)
ffff0010:	b9400000 	ldr	w0, [x0]
ffff0014:	12001400 	and	w0, w0, #0x3f
ffff0018:	34000220 	cbz	w0, 0xffff005c		; jump here if any of reset flags are set

; Which core are we ?
; mpidr_el1 is the "multiprocessor affinity register"
; reading it lets us determine which core is running.
; The low 8 bits tell us which core (0,1,2,3) in a cluster
; The next 8 bits enable to to determine which cluster
ffff001c:	d53800a0 	mrs	x0, mpidr_el1
ffff0020:	92402000 	and	x0, x0, #0x1ff
ffff0024:	f100001f 	cmp	x0, #0x0
ffff0028:	54000200 	b.eq	0xffff0068

; We are some core other than cluster 0, core 0
; Cores other than cluster 0, core 0 will spin here.
; The mechanism for awakening one of these cores is as follows:
;
; Write the address to run to 0xff090008 (in SRAM)
; Write 0xdeadbeaf to 0xff090004 (in SRAM)
;
; Note that we write 0xdeadbeaf, not 0xdeadbeef
;
; It seems to me that this will wake up all the cores and
; take them to this other address, which will then have to
; sort them all out via the mpidr_el1 register.
; It is a little surprising that we cannot select a single core
; to bring out of this WFE spin loop, but it is what it is.
ffff002c:	580006e0 	ldr	x0, 0xffff0108	; x0 = ff090004
ffff0030:	52800021 	mov	w1, #0x1 		; w1 = 1
ffff0034:	b9000001 	str	w1, [x0]		; [ff090004] = 1
ffff0038:	d503205f 	wfe
ffff003c:	180006e2 	ldr	w2, 0xffff0118	; w2 = 0xdeadbeaf
ffff0040:	58000640 	ldr	x0, 0xffff0108	; ff090004
ffff0044:	b9400001 	ldr	w1, [x0]		; w1 = [ff090004]
ffff0048:	6b02003f 	cmp	w1, w2
ffff004c:	54ffff61 	b.ne	0xffff0038  ; w1 != w2, go back to sleep

ffff0050:	58000601 	ldr	x1, 0xffff0110	; x1 = ff090008
ffff0054:	b9400020 	ldr	w0, [x1]		; w1 = [ff090008] = core start address
ffff0058:	d61f0000 	br	x0 				; go run at that address

; Mystery register code continues here
; and we have another mystery register from SGRF space
; This is some mechanism to intercept a core coming out of reset
; without having it parked in the spin loop above.
ffff005c:	580006a1 	ldr	x1, 0xffff0130	; ff0d0180 (SGRF - another magic register)
ffff0060:	b9400020 	ldr	w0, [x1]		; get start address from that register
ffff0064:	d61f0000 	br	x0				; go run at that address

; cluster 0, core 0 comes here.
; This is the mainstream startup thread.
;
; zero each and every register
ffff0068:	aa1f03e0 	mov	x0, xzr
ffff006c:	aa1f03e1 	mov	x1, xzr
ffff0070:	aa1f03e2 	mov	x2, xzr
ffff0074:	aa1f03e3 	mov	x3, xzr
ffff0078:	aa1f03e4 	mov	x4, xzr
ffff007c:	aa1f03e5 	mov	x5, xzr
ffff0080:	aa1f03e6 	mov	x6, xzr
ffff0084:	aa1f03e7 	mov	x7, xzr
ffff0088:	aa1f03e8 	mov	x8, xzr
ffff008c:	aa1f03e9 	mov	x9, xzr
ffff0090:	aa1f03ea 	mov	x10, xzr
ffff0094:	aa1f03eb 	mov	x11, xzr
ffff0098:	aa1f03ec 	mov	x12, xzr
ffff009c:	aa1f03ed 	mov	x13, xzr
ffff00a0:	aa1f03ee 	mov	x14, xzr
ffff00a4:	aa1f03ef 	mov	x15, xzr
ffff00a8:	aa1f03f0 	mov	x16, xzr
ffff00ac:	aa1f03f1 	mov	x17, xzr
ffff00b0:	aa1f03f2 	mov	x18, xzr
ffff00b4:	aa1f03f3 	mov	x19, xzr
ffff00b8:	aa1f03f4 	mov	x20, xzr
ffff00bc:	aa1f03f5 	mov	x21, xzr
ffff00c0:	aa1f03f6 	mov	x22, xzr
ffff00c4:	aa1f03f7 	mov	x23, xzr
ffff00c8:	aa1f03f8 	mov	x24, xzr
ffff00cc:	aa1f03f9 	mov	x25, xzr
ffff00d0:	aa1f03fa 	mov	x26, xzr
ffff00d4:	aa1f03fb 	mov	x27, xzr
ffff00d8:	aa1f03fc 	mov	x28, xzr
ffff00dc:	aa1f03fd 	mov	x29, xzr
ffff00e0:	aa1f03fe 	mov	x30, xzr

; initialize our stack
ffff00e4:	580000a0 	ldr	x0, 0xffff00f8	; ff091000
ffff00e8:	9100001f 	mov	sp, x0

; Initialize our vector table
ffff00ec:	580000a0 	ldr	x0, 0xffff0100	; ffff0800
ffff00f0:	d51ec000 	msr	vbar_el3, x0

; Go
ffff00f4:	14001065 	b	0xffff4288

; Here are a bunch of 64 bit constants referenced above.

ffff00f8:	00000000 ff091000 		; this loads to the SP
ffff0100:	00000000 ffff0800 		; this points to a vector table
ffff0108:	00000000 ff090004 		; address to write the start constant to release the cores
ffff0110:	00000000 ff090008 		; start address for other cores
ffff0118:	00000000 deadbeaf 		; start constant for other cores
ffff0120:	00000000 ff440094
ffff0128:	00000000 ff0d0000 		; magic redister in SGRF
ffff0130:	00000000 ff0d0180 		; 2-nd magic redister in SGRF

; subroutine, called from below
; x0 - count?, seems only 1 and 2 are valid values
; x1 - buff pointer
ffff0138:	d10103ff 	sub	sp, sp, #0x40
ffff013c:	a9037bfd 	stp	x29, x30, [sp, #48]
ffff0140:	9100c3fd 	add	x29, sp, #0x30
ffff0144:	a9025ff6 	stp	x22, x23, [sp, #32]
ffff0148:	a90157f4 	stp	x20, x21, [sp, #16]
ffff014c:	f90007f3 	str	x19, [sp, #8]			; preserve x19

ffff0150:	aa0103f3 	mov	x19, x1					; buff
ffff0154:	aa0003f4 	mov	x20, x0					; arguments to w19, w20

ffff0158:	b90007ff 	str	wzr, [sp, #4]			; _sp4 = 0
ffff015c:	52800020 	mov	w0, #0x1				; return value in w0
ffff0160:	b4000353 	cbz	x19, 0xffff01c8			; return error if buff == NULL
ffff0164:	52800275 	mov	w21, #0x13
ffff0168:	7100069f 	cmp	w20, #0x1
ffff016c:	54000080 	b.eq	0xffff017c			; skip to below if count == 1
ffff0170:	71000a9f 	cmp	w20, #0x2
ffff0174:	540002a1 	b.ne	0xffff01c8			; return error if count != 2
ffff0178:	52800295 	mov	w21, #0x14
; fall through here if count == 1 or 2, also
;   w21 = 0x13 if count == 1
;   w21 = 0x14 if count == 2
ffff017c:	2a1f03f6 	mov	w22, wzr
ffff0180:	2a1f03f7 	mov	w23, wzr				; i = 0
; loop_i
ffff0184:	0b3542e1 	add	w1, w23, w21, uxtw		; w1 = i + (0x13 or 0x14)
ffff0188:	910013e0 	add	x0, sp, #0x4
ffff018c:	52800022 	mov	w2, #0x1				; read 1 word
ffff0190:	94000f95 	bl	0xffff3fe4				; read_efuse()
ffff0194:	b94007e0 	ldr	w0, [sp, #4]			; get result in w0
ffff0198:	2a1f03e1 	mov	w1, wzr					; w1 = 0
ffff019c:	340000a0 	cbz	w0, 0xffff01b0			; skip below loop if w0 == 0
; loop
ffff01a0:	51000402 	sub	w2, w0, #0x1
ffff01a4:	0a000040 	and	w0, w2, w0				; w0 = w0 & (w0 - 1) ???
ffff01a8:	11000421 	add	w1, w1, #0x1			; w1++, accumulate num iterations here
ffff01ac:	35ffffa0 	cbnz	w0, 0xffff01a0		; loop until w0 != 0
; 
ffff01b0:	0b364036 	add	w22, w1, w22, uxtw		; w22 += w1
ffff01b4:	110006f7 	add	w23, w23, #0x1			; i++
ffff01b8:	6b3442ff 	cmp	w23, w20, uxtw
ffff01bc:	54fffe41 	b.ne	0xffff0184			; loop_ while i != count

ffff01c0:	2a1f03e0 	mov	w0, wzr					; return OK
ffff01c4:	b9000276 	str	w22, [x19]				; *buff = w22
; done

ffff0278:	94000fe3 	bl	0xffff4204
ffff027c:	350000e0 	cbnz	w0, 0xffff0298
ffff0280:	4b394294 	sub	w20, w20, w25, uxtw

; ==============================================================
; Many calls to this routine.
; I will call it "xxdelay()"
;
; it is commonly called with xxdelay(1)
; but values of 10, 100, 500, 1000, 2000 are used.
;
; In essence it is some kind of variable delay loop
; that depends on the argument in w0 as well as
; the value stored in SRAM + 0 (the cpu speed in Mhz)
;
; As near as I can tell, only 3 values are ever stored into SRAM[0]
; either 24 or 200 or 400
; so the first branch below is used when SRAM[0] = 24 and xxdelay(1)
;  is the call, i.e. for a very short delay.
;
; The "madd" instruction is the star of the show
; this is the only place in this code that "madd" is used.
; In the code here, it essentially does:
;     w0 = w1 + w0 * w2
ffff06b0:	d10043ff 	sub	sp, sp, #0x10
ffff06b4:	a9007bfd 	stp	x29, x30, [sp]
ffff06b8:	910003fd 	mov	x29, sp

; get cpu speed
ffff06bc:	90ff8501 	adrp	x1, 0xff090000		; SRAM
ffff06c0:	b9400021 	ldr	w1, [x1]				; w1 = [SRAM + 0]
ffff06c4:	1b007c20 	mul	w0, w1, w0

; Here we have calculated w0 = arg*mhz
; We compare the cpu mhz to 0x18 (24)
; if it matches, w0 = (0x1fff + w0 * 0x75) >> 13
; otherwise ---  w0 = (0x7fff + w0 * 0xb9) >> 15
; if this result is non-zero, we use it to delay
;
; Just out of curiosity, what happens when this is called with xxdelay(1)
; and the cpu speed is 24?  We calculate 0x1fff + 24 * 117 = 10999
; We shift this right 13 bits to get 1
; This goes to a simple delay that will execute 2 instructions
; per count.  This seems like pretty short delay.
; What if the cpu speed is 400 and we ask for a delay of 10
; calculate 32767 + 4000 * 185 = 772767
; shift this by 2 to get 23, still a very short delay
ffff06c8:	7100603f 	cmp	w1, #0x18
ffff06cc:	540000c1 	b.ne	0xffff06e4  // b.any
ffff06d0:	5283ffe1 	mov	w1, #0x1fff                	// #8191
ffff06d4:	52800ee2 	mov	w2, #0x77                  	// #119
ffff06d8:	1b020400 	madd	w0, w0, w2, w1
ffff06dc:	530d7c00 	lsr	w0, w0, #13
ffff06e0:	14000004 	b	0xffff06f0
ffff06e4:	528fffe1 	mov	w1, #0x7fff                	// #32767
ffff06e8:	0b001c20 	add	w0, w1, w0, lsl #7
ffff06ec:	530f7c00 	lsr	w0, w0, #15
ffff06f0:	34000040 	cbz	w0, 0xffff06f8
ffff06f4:	94000fb9 	bl	0xffff45d8				; call delay loop on x0
ffff06f8:	a9407bfd 	ldp	x29, x30, [sp]
ffff06fc:	910043ff 	add	sp, sp, #0x10
ffff0700:	d65f03c0 	ret

; subroutine mmdelay ( count )
; x0 is argument, and is count of how many times
; to make calls to xxdelay(1000)
ffff0704:	d10083ff 	sub	sp, sp, #0x20
ffff0708:	a9017bfd 	stp	x29, x30, [sp, #16]
ffff070c:	910043fd 	add	x29, sp, #0x10
ffff0710:	f90007f3 	str	x19, [sp, #8]

ffff0714:	aa0003f3 	mov	x19, x0					; x19 = arg
ffff0718:	340000b3 	cbz	w19, 0xffff072c
ffff071c:	51000673 	sub	w19, w19, #0x1			; x19--
ffff0720:	52807d00 	mov	w0, #0x3e8				; w0 = 1000
ffff0724:	97ffffe3 	bl	0xffff06b0				; xxdelay ( 1000 )
ffff0728:	35ffffb3 	cbnz	w19, 0xffff071c

ffff072c:	f94007f3 	ldr	x19, [sp, #8]
ffff0730:	a9417bfd 	ldp	x29, x30, [sp, #16]
ffff0734:	910083ff 	add	sp, sp, #0x20
ffff0738:	d65f03c0 	ret

; some constants used from code
ffff073c:	4b415352								; "RSAK"

; VBAR_EL3 points to this
; This is exception vector table for EL3
; There are dedicated vector tables for each exception level:
;  * VBAR_EL3.
;  * VBAR_EL2.
;  * VBAR_EL1.
; The vector table in AArch64 is different from that in AArch32. The vector table in AArch64
; mode contains 16 entries. Each entry is 128B in size and contains at most 32 instructions.
; Vector tables must be placed at a 2KB-aligned address. The addresses are specified by
; initializing VBAR_ELn registers.
ffff0800:	14000000 	b	0xffff0800
ffff0804:	d503201f 	nop
	.....
	.....
	.....
ffff087c:	d503201f 	nop
ffff0880:	14000000 	b	0xffff0880
ffff0884:	d503201f 	nop
	.....
	.....
	.....
ffff08fc:	d503201f 	nop
ffff0900:	14000000 	b	0xffff0900
ffff0904:	d503201f 	nop
	.....
	.....
	.....
ffff097c:	d503201f 	nop
ffff0980:	14000000 	b	0xffff0980
ffff0984:	d503201f 	nop
	.....
	.....
	.....
ffff09fc:	d503201f 	nop

; This is exception vector and is not referenced anywhere.
; The exception handler for the synchronous exception from the
; current EL using the current SP.
; basically do this: elr_el3 += 4, so it seems like next
; EL will be switched to EL2 from next instruction
ffff0a00:	f81f0fe0 	str	x0, [sp, #-16]!			; save x0
ffff0a04:	d53e4020 	mrs	x0, elr_el3				; read elr_el3
ffff0a08:	91001000 	add	x0, x0, #0x4			; add 4 to it
ffff0a0c:	d51e4020 	msr	elr_el3, x0				; write elr_el3
ffff0a10:	f84107e0 	ldr	x0, [sp], #16			; restore x0
ffff0a14:	d69f03e0 	eret						; return from exception

ffff0a18:	d503201f 	nop
	.....
	.....
	.....
ffff0a7c:	d503201f 	nop

; another interrupt handler
; The exception handler for the IRQ exception from the
; current EL using the current SP.
ffff0a80:	a9bf07e0 	stp	x0, x1, [sp, #-16]!		; store registers
ffff0a84:	a9bf0fe2 	stp	x2, x3, [sp, #-16]!

ffff0ad0:	a8c107e0 	ldp	x0, x1, [sp], #16
ffff0ad4:	d69f03e0 	eret						; return from interrupt

ffff0ad8:	d503201f 	nop
	.....

;  =======================================
; Base address 0xff060000 is crypto

; Here is what I see in "crypto_set_hash_len () which follows

;  crypt + 0xc |= 0x20
;    crypt + 0x188 = w0
;  crypt + 0x180 = 0xa
;  crypt + 0x8 = 0x40_0040
;  spin until crypt + 0x8 goes 0
;  return 0

; Now, looking at a driver for the rk3328 crypto
; c + 0xc is the config register, Crypto_CONF, the above sets "address mode"
; c + 0x8 is the control register, Crypto_CTRL,
;	the above sets "hash flush"
;	note the business of the top 16 bits being an enable mask
; c + 0x0180 is the hash control register, Crypto_HASH_CTRL
; c + 0x0188 is the hash length register, Crypto_HASH_MSG_LEN


;  =======================================

; subroutine crypto_set_hash_len ( length )

; set the length in bytes - 2 of something that will follow
ffff0b84:	90000021 	adrp	x1, 0xffff4000
ffff0b88:	b948e421 	ldr	w1, [x1, #2276]			; ffff48e4: ff06000c, Crypto_CONF
ffff0b8c:	b9400022 	ldr	w2, [x1]
ffff0b90:	321b0042 	orr	w2, w2, #0x20
ffff0b94:	b9000022 	str	w2, [x1]				; Crypto_CONF |= 0x20

ffff0b98:	90000021 	adrp	x1, 0xffff4000
ffff0b9c:	b948e821 	ldr	w1, [x1, #2280]			; ffff48e8: ff060188, Crypto_HASH_MSG_LEN
ffff0ba0:	b9000020 	str	w0, [x1]				; Crypto_HASH_MSG_LEN = arg

ffff0ba4:	90000020 	adrp	x0, 0xffff4000
ffff0ba8:	b948ec01 	ldr	w1, [x0, #2284]			; ffff48ec: ff060180, Crypto_HASH_CTRL
ffff0bac:	90000020 	adrp	x0, 0xffff4000
ffff0bb0:	b948f000 	ldr	w0, [x0, #2288]			; ffff48f0: ff060008, Crypto_CTRL

ffff0bb4:	52800142 	mov	w2, #0xa
ffff0bb8:	b9000022 	str	w2, [x1]				; Crypto_HASH_CTRL = 0xa

ffff0bbc:	320a83e1 	mov	w1, #0x400040
ffff0bc0:	b9000001 	str	w1, [x0]				; Crypto_CTRL = 0x40_0040

ffff0bc4:	b9400001 	ldr	w1, [x0]
ffff0bc8:	3737ffe1 	tbnz	w1, #6, 0xffff0bc4	; loop on ctl, bit 0x40
ffff0bcc:	aa1f03e0 	mov	x0, xzr					; return 0 (always)
ffff0bd0:	d65f03c0 	ret

;  =======================================

; subroutine crypto2 ( buf, len )

; spin reading control register until bit 3 (0x08) clears
; this bit is "hash start"

; subroutine, called once during startup.
; Writing a variety of stuff to ff44 addresses (CRU)
; CRU is the clock and reset unit

; =======================================================
; subroutine, called from 2 places
; setup clock and reset signals
ffff194c:	d100c3ff 	sub	sp, sp, #0x30
ffff1950:	a9027bfd 	stp	x29, x30, [sp, #32]
ffff1954:	910083fd 	add	x29, sp, #0x20
ffff1958:	a9015bf5 	stp	x21, x22, [sp, #16]
ffff195c:	a90053f3 	stp	x19, x20, [sp]

ffff1960:	97fffb4e 	bl	0xffff0698				; get cpu clock speed in w0
ffff1964:	71031c1f 	cmp	w0, #0xc7
ffff1968:	54000908 	b.hi	0xffff1a88			; > 199 MHz?

; set the CRU_APLL_CON1 register to 0x20002000
; as always the upper 16 bits are a mask,
; so we are changing bit 13 only which is PLL 
; global power down, presumably this powers down
; the PLL, not the entire SoC.
; second writing of 0x10001000 means PLL delta
; sigma modulator disable
;
; NOTE that x20 holds a pointer to CRU_APLL_CON1 and
; x21 points to CRU_GPLL_CON1 to the end of the routine
ffff196c:	f0000000 	adrp	x0, 0xffff4000
ffff1970:	b94a0014 	ldr	w20, [x0, #2560]		; ffff4a00: ff440004, x20 = &CRU_APLL_CON1
ffff1974:	320383e0 	mov	w0, #0x20002000
ffff1978:	b9000280 	str	w0, [x20]				; CRU_APLL_CON1 = 0x20002000
ffff197c:	320483f3 	mov	w19, #0x10001000
ffff1980:	b9000293 	str	w19, [x20]				; CRU_APLL_CON1 = 0x10001000
ffff1984:	f0000000 	adrp	x0, 0xffff4000

; Now set the value in CRU_APLL_CON0
; fbdiv = 0xc8 = 200 (reset value is 0x64)
; postdiv1 = 4
ffff1988:	b94a0400 	ldr	w0, [x0, #2564]			; ffff4a04: 7fff40c8
ffff198c:	d2bfe881 	mov	x1, #0xff440000
ffff1990:	b9000020 	str	w0, [x1]				; CRU_APLL_CON0 = 0x7fff40c8

; CRU_APLL_CON1 = 0x01ff0043
; postdiv2 = 1
; refdiv = 3
ffff1994:	f0000000 	adrp	x0, 0xffff4000
ffff1998:	b94a0800 	ldr	w0, [x0, #2568]			; ffff4a08: 01ff0043
ffff199c:	b9000280 	str	w0, [x20]				; CRU_APLL_CON1 = 0x01ff0043

; get a pointer to CRU_GPLL_CON1 in x21
ffff19a0:	f0000000 	adrp	x0, 0xffff4000
ffff19a4:	b94a0c15 	ldr	w21, [x0, #2572]		; ffff4a0c: ff440064, x21 = &CRU_GPLL_CON1
ffff19a8:	52a40016 	mov	w22, #0x20000000
ffff19ac:	f0000000 	adrp	x0, 0xffff4000
ffff19b0:	b94a1000 	ldr	w0, [x0, #2576]			; ffff4a10: 7fff30c0
ffff19b4:	f0000001 	adrp	x1, 0xffff4000
ffff19b8:	b94a1421 	ldr	w1, [x1, #2580]			; ffff4a14: ff440060, x1 = &CRU_GPLL_CON0

ffff19bc:	f0000002 	adrp	x2, 0xffff4000
ffff19c0:	b94a1842 	ldr	w2, [x2, #2584]			; ffff4a18: 01ff0042

; get GPLL
ffff19c4:	b90002b6 	str	w22, [x21]				; CRU_GPLL_CON1 = 0x20000000
ffff19c8:	b90002b3 	str	w19, [x21]				; CRU_GPLL_CON1 = 0x10001000
ffff19cc:	b9000020 	str	w0, [x1]				; CRU_GPLL_CON0 = 0x7fff30c0
ffff19d0:	b90002a2 	str	w2, [x21]				; CRU_GPLL_CON1 = 0x01ff0042
ffff19d4:	52800140 	mov	w0, #0xa
ffff19d8:	97fffb36 	bl	0xffff06b0				; xxdelay(10)

; enable APPL and GPLL
ffff19dc:	b9000296 	str	w22, [x20]				; CRU_APLL_CON1 = 0x20000000
ffff19e0:	b90002b6 	str	w22, [x21]				; CRU_GPLL_CON1 = 0x20000000
ffff19e4:	52807d00 	mov	w0, #0x3e8
ffff19e8:	97fffb32 	bl	0xffff06b0				; xxdelay(1000)

; now read the "global reset status" register
; CRU_GLB_RST_ST (trm, page 44(
; mask out the low (and only) 6 bits.
; if they are zero set CPU at 400
; otherwise fall through and set 200
ffff19ec:	f0000000 	adrp	x0, 0xffff4000
ffff19f0:	b94a1c00 	ldr	w0, [x0, #2588]			; ffff4a1c: 0xff440094, x0 = &CRU_GLB_RST_ST
ffff19f4:	b9400000 	ldr	w0, [x0]
ffff19f8:	12001400 	and	w0, w0, #0x3f			; w0 = CRU_GLB_RST_ST & 0x3f
ffff19fc:	34000100 	cbz	w0, 0xffff1a1c

; Write to CRU_CLKSEL_CON0 (trm, p. 48)
; We write 7fdf2501
; the mask is 0111_1111_1101_1111
; a 2 bit field = 1 -- bus_aclk_pll_sel (GPLL)
; a 5 bit field = 5 -- bus_aclk_div_con (clk=src/(div+1))
; a 2 bit field = 0 -- core_clk_pll_sel = APLL
; a 5 bit field = 1 -- clk_core_div_con
; setting 1 in the last field gets a divide by 2.
ffff1a00:	f0000000 	adrp	x0, 0xffff4000
ffff1a04:	b94a2000 	ldr	w0, [x0, #2592]			; ffff4a20: 7fdf2501
ffff1a08:	f0000001 	adrp	x1, 0xffff4000
ffff1a0c:	b94a2421 	ldr	w1, [x1, #2596]			; ffff4a24: ff440100, x1 = &CRU_CLKSEL_CON0
ffff1a10:	b9000020 	str	w0, [x1]				; CRU_CLKSEL_CON0 = 0x7fdf2501
ffff1a14:	52801900 	mov	w0, #0xc8				; declare CPU at 200 MHz
ffff1a18:	14000007 	b	0xffff1a34

; same as above, the only difference is the last field
; where we set the div to 0 (yielding a divide by 1)
ffff1a1c:	f0000000 	adrp	x0, 0xffff4000
ffff1a20:	b94a2800 	ldr	w0, [x0, #2600]			; ffff4a28: 7fdf2500
ffff1a24:	f0000001 	adrp	x1, 0xffff4000
ffff1a28:	b94a2421 	ldr	w1, [x1, #2596]			; ffff4a24: ff440100, x1 = &CRU_CLKSEL_CON0
ffff1a2c:	b9000020 	str	w0, [x1]				; CRU_CLKSEL_CON0 = 0x7fdf2500
ffff1a30:	52803200 	mov	w0, #0x190				; declare CPU at 400 MHz

; CRU_CLKSEL_CON1 = 0x737f1113
;   bus_pclk_div_con = 1 (clk=src/div_con+1)
;   bus_hclk_div_con = 1 (clk=src/div_con+1)
;   aclk_core_div_con = 1 (clk=src/div_con+1)
;   clk_core_dbg_div_con = 3 (clk=src/div_con+1)
; CRU_CLKSEL_CON28 = 0x00df0043
;   periph_pll_sel = 1 (GPLL)
;   periph_aclk_div_con = 4 (clk=src/div_con+1)
; CRU_CLKSEL_CON29 = 0x00733001
;   periph_pclk_div_con = 0 (clk=src/div_con+1)
;   periph_hclk_div_con = 1 (clk=src/div_con+1)
; CRU_CRU_MODE = 0x00010001
;   apll_work_mode = 1, Normal mode, clock from PLL output
; CRU_CRU_MODE = 0x10001000
;   gpll_work_mode = 1, Normal mode, clock from PLL output
ffff1a34:	f0000001 	adrp	x1, 0xffff4000
ffff1a38:	b94a2c21 	ldr	w1, [x1, #2604]			; ffff4a2c: 737f1113
ffff1a3c:	f0000002 	adrp	x2, 0xffff4000
ffff1a40:	b94a3042 	ldr	w2, [x2, #2608]			; ffff4a30: ff440104, x2 = &CRU_CLKSEL_CON1
ffff1a44:	b9000041 	str	w1, [x2]				; CRU_CLKSEL_CON1 = 0x737f1113
ffff1a48:	f0000001 	adrp	x1, 0xffff4000
ffff1a4c:	b94a3421 	ldr	w1, [x1, #2612]			; ffff4a34: 00df0043
ffff1a50:	f0000002 	adrp	x2, 0xffff4000
ffff1a54:	b94a3842 	ldr	w2, [x2, #2616]			; ffff4a38: ff440170, x2 = &CRU_CLKSEL_CON28
ffff1a58:	b9000041 	str	w1, [x2]				; CRU_CLKSEL_CON28 = 0x00df0043
ffff1a5c:	f0000001 	adrp	x1, 0xffff4000
ffff1a60:	b94a3c21 	ldr	w1, [x1, #2620]			; ffff4a3c: 00733001
ffff1a64:	f0000002 	adrp	x2, 0xffff4000
ffff1a68:	b94a4042 	ldr	w2, [x2, #2624]			; ffff4a40: ff440174, x2 = &CRU_CLKSEL_CON29
ffff1a6c:	f0000003 	adrp	x3, 0xffff4000
ffff1a70:	b94a4463 	ldr	w3, [x3, #2628]			; ffff4a44: ff440080, x3 = &CRU_CRU_MODE
ffff1a74:	b9000041 	str	w1, [x2]				; CRU_CLKSEL_CON29 = 0x00733001
ffff1a78:	320083e1 	mov	w1, #0x10001
ffff1a7c:	b9000061 	str	w1, [x3]				; CRU_CRU_MODE = 0x00010001
ffff1a80:	b9000073 	str	w19, [x3]				; CRU_CRU_MODE = 0x10001000
ffff1a84:	97fffb08 	bl	0xffff06a4
; jump here if clock is > 199MHz
ffff1a88:	a94053f3 	ldp	x19, x20, [sp]
ffff1a8c:	a9415bf5 	ldp	x21, x22, [sp, #16]
ffff1a90:	a9427bfd 	ldp	x29, x30, [sp, #32]
ffff1a94:	9100c3ff 	add	sp, sp, #0x30
ffff1a98:	d65f03c0 	ret

;	ffff4600:	00000000 ffff220c					; probably init()

; usb_set_address(), single call from ffff3bc4

ffff2eb8:	b94b1400 	ldr	w0, [x0, #2836]			; x0 = ffff4b14: ff580800, &USBOTG_DCFG

; We handle either "device" or "configuration"
;  configuration is "2' which we handle immediately below
;  "device" is "1" and we branch to verify that and
;  if so to handle it.

; so, we have a get descriptor, of type "configuration"

ffff2f24:	2a012001 	orr	w1, w0, w1, lsl #8		; 16 bit count field from usb[6..7]

; len = len < 10 ? len : 32
; so we will send at least 10 bytes
ffff2f28:	7100283f 	cmp	w1, #0xa
ffff2f2c:	d0ff84f4 	adrp	x20, 0xff090000
ffff2f30:	f9405280 	ldr	x0, [x20, #160]			; x0 = SRAM[160], pointer sram-J
ffff2f34:	52800402 	mov	w2, #0x20

ffff2f3c:	53007e62 	lsr	w2, w19, #0				; w2 = usb[6..7] < 10 ? usb[6..7] : 32
ffff2f40:	d0000001 	adrp	x1, 0xffff4000
ffff2f44:	911af021 	add	x1, x1, #0x6bc			; x1 = ffff46bc: 00200209
ffff2f48:	94000515 	bl	0xffff439c				; memcpy(SRAM[160], ffff46bc, w2)

; So we are going to send the following 32 bytes when we branch:
; ---- ffff46bc:	09 02 20 00 01 01 00 80
; ---- ffff46c4:	c8 09 04 00 00 02 ff 06
; ---- ffff46cc:	05 00 07 05 81 02 00 02
; ---- ffff46d4:	00 07 05 02 02 00 02 00

; What are they?
; They are actually a lump of 4 descriptors, as follows:

;  -- the first 9 bytes:
; The first byte is a length (9 bytes)
; The next byte is the descriptor type = 2 (configuration)
; the next word is total length in bytes = 0x0020
; The next byte is the number of interfaces = 1
; The next byte is config value = 1
; The next byte is a string index (0, not used)
; The next byte is an attribute bitmap = 0x80 (nothing here)
; The next byte is max power = 0xc8 = 200 mA

;  -- the next 9 bytes:
; The first byte is a length (9 bytes)
; The next byte is the descriptor type = 4 (interface)
; The next byte is the interface number = 0
; The next byte is the "alternate setting" = 0
; The next byte is "number of endpoints" = 2
; The next byte is "interface class" = 0xff = vendor defined
; The next byte is "subclass" = 6
; The next byte is "protocol" = 5
; The next byte is string index for interface = 0

;  -- the next 7 bytes:
; The first byte is a length (7 bytes)
; The next byte is the descriptor type = 5 (endpoint descr)
; The next byte is endpoint address = 0x81 (IN 1)
; The next byte is attributes = 2 (bulk)
; The next word is max packet size = 0x200 = 512
; The next byte is polling interval = 0

;  -- the next 7 bytes:
; The first byte is a length (7 bytes)
; The next byte is the descriptor type = 5 (endpoint descr)
; The next byte is endpoint address = 0x02 (OUT 2)
; The next byte is attributes = 2 (bulk)
; The next word is max packet size = 0x200 = 512
; The next byte is polling interval = 0

; --------------------------
; We got a SETUP packet with "SET CONFIGURATION"

ffff2f4c:	d0ff84e0 	adrp	x0, 0xff090000
ffff2f50:	79410000 	ldrh	w0, [x0, #128]
ffff2f54:	7101001f 	cmp	w0, #0x40				; SRAM[128] == 0x40?
ffff2f58:	540000e1 	b.ne	0xffff2f74			; branch if not

; SRAM[128] == 0x40, set some 16 bit counter fields in sram
ffff2f5c:	f9405280 	ldr	x0, [x20, #160]			; x0 = SRAM[160], pointer to some ram where usb data are
ffff2f60:	39005c1f 	strb	wzr, [x0, #23]		; x0[23] = 0
ffff2f64:	52800801 	mov	w1, #0x40
ffff2f68:	39005801 	strb	w1, [x0, #22]		; x0[22] = 0x40
ffff2f6c:	3900781f 	strb	wzr, [x0, #30]		; x0[30] = 0
ffff2f70:	39007401 	strb	w1, [x0, #29]		; x0[29] = 0x40

; SRAM[128] != 0x40
ffff2f74:	f9405281 	ldr	x1, [x20, #160]			; x1 = SRAM[160]
ffff2f78:	14000018 	b	0xffff2fd8				; send usb data

; came here if usb[3] != 2
; We have a SETUP packet of "get descriptor", not configuration
; Check the type code, if it is 1,
; we have "get descriptor" of type "device" ****

; this is in general the first packet we see.
; we set up a response that includes the vendor/product code
ffff2f7c:	7100043f 	cmp	w1, #0x1				; is it 1 ?
ffff2f80:	54000321 	b.ne	0xffff2fe4			; no

; usb[3] == 1
ffff2f84:	39401c01 	ldrb	w1, [x0, #7]		; J+7
ffff2f88:	39401800 	ldrb	w0, [x0, #6]		; J+8
ffff2f8c:	2a012001 	orr	w1, w0, w1, lsl #8		; 16 bit count field from usb[6..7]

; len = len > 18 ? 18 : len
ffff2f90:	7100483f 	cmp	w1, #0x12
ffff2f94:	d0ff84f4 	adrp	x20, 0xff090000
ffff2f98:	f9405280 	ldr	x0, [x20, #160]			; x0 = SRAM[160], pointer sram-J
ffff2f9c:	52800242 	mov	w2, #0x12
ffff2fa0:	1a818053 	csel	w19, w2, w1, hi
ffff2fa4:	53007e62 	lsr	w2, w19, #0				; w2 = usb[6..7] > 18 ? 18 : usb[6..7]
ffff2fa8:	d0000001 	adrp	x1, 0xffff4000
ffff2fac:	911aa021 	add	x1, x1, #0x6a8			; x1 = ffff46a8: 02000112
ffff2fb0:	940004fb 	bl	0xffff439c				; memcpy(SRAM[160], ffff46a8, w2)

; The following lump of stuff includes
;  the vendor/ product code.
; So we must be responding to a setup packet
; of some kind.

; --- ffff46a8:	12 01 00 02 00 00 00 40
; --- ffff46b0:	07 22 0c 32 00 01 00 00
; --- ffff46b8:	00 01 00 00 09 02 20 00

; The first byte is the length = 0x12 = 18 (sounds right)
; The next byte is the descriptor type = 0x01 for device descriptor
; The next word is the USB spec we are following in BCD = 0x0002 = 2.0
; The next byte is "device class" = 0
; The next byte is "device subclass" = 0
; The next byte is "device protocol" = 0
; The next byte is "max packet size" = 0x40 = 64
; The next word is "vendor" = 0722 --> 2207 = Rockchip
; The next word is "product" = 0c32 --> 320c = RK3328
; The next word is "device release number" in BCD = 1.0
; The next byte is an index for manufacturer string (0=no string)
; The next byte is an index for product string (0=no string)

; The next byte is a serial number = 0
; The next byte is the number of configs = 1
ffff2fb4:	d0ff84e0 	adrp	x0, 0xff090000
ffff2fb8:	79410000 	ldrh	w0, [x0, #128]
ffff2fbc:	f9405281 	ldr	x1, [x20, #160]			; x1 = SRAM[160], pointer to some ram where usb data are
ffff2fc0:	7101001f 	cmp	w0, #0x40				; SRAM[128] == 0x40 ?
ffff2fc4:	540000a1 	b.ne	0xffff2fd8			; no - send usb packet
; SRAM[128] == 0x40
ffff2fc8:	52800020 	mov	w0, #0x1
ffff2fcc:	39000c20 	strb	w0, [x1, #3]		; x1[3] = 1
ffff2fd0:	52800200 	mov	w0, #0x10
ffff2fd4:	39000820 	strb	w0, [x1, #2]		; x1[2] = 16
; send usb data
ffff2fd8:	aa1303e0 	mov	x0, x19
ffff2fdc:	940000c6 	bl	0xffff32f4				; usb_oep0_put_data(count, SRAM[160])
ffff2fe0:	1400000b 	b	0xffff300c				; return

; usb[3] is neither 1 nor 2
ffff2fe4:	d0000000 	adrp	x0, 0xffff4000
ffff2fe8:	b94b1800 	ldr	w0, [x0, #2840]			; x0 = ffff4b18: ff580b00, &USBOTG_DOEPCTL0
ffff2fec:	b9400001 	ldr	w1, [x0]
ffff2ff0:	d0000002 	adrp	x2, 0xffff4000
ffff2ff4:	b94b1c42 	ldr	w2, [x2, #2844]			; x2 = ffff4b1c: ff580900, &USBOTG_DIEPCTL0
ffff2ff8:	320b0021 	orr	w1, w1, #0x200000
ffff2ffc:	b9000001 	str	w1, [x0]				; USBOTG_DOEPCTL0 |= 0x200000, STALL Handshake
ffff3000:	b9400040 	ldr	w0, [x2]
ffff3004:	320b0000 	orr	w0, w0, #0x200000
ffff3008:	b9000040 	str	w0, [x2]				; USBOTG_DIEPCTL0 |= 0x200000, STALL Handshake
; done
ffff300c:	a94053f3 	ldp	x19, x20, [sp]
ffff3010:	a9417bfd 	ldp	x29, x30, [sp, #16]
ffff3014:	910083ff 	add	sp, sp, #0x20
ffff3018:	d65f03c0 	ret

; usb_stall(), single call
; set STALL bit for IN and OUT EP0, core will clear this bit
; when SETUP token is received for the corresponding endpoint
ffff301c:	b0000000 	adrp	x0, 0xffff4000
ffff3020:	b94b2000 	ldr	w0, [x0, #2848]			; x0 = ffff4b20: ff580b00, &USBOTG_DOEPCTL0
ffff3024:	b9400001 	ldr	w1, [x0]
ffff3028:	b0000002 	adrp	x2, 0xffff4000

ffff3030:	320b0021 	orr	w1, w1, #0x200000
ffff3034:	b9000001 	str	w1, [x0]				; USBOTG_DOEPCTL0 |= 0x200000, STALL Handshake
ffff3038:	b9400040 	ldr	w0, [x2]
ffff303c:	320b0000 	orr	w0, w0, #0x200000
ffff3040:	b9000040 	str	w0, [x2]				; USBOTG_DIEPCTL0 |= 0x200000, STALL Handshake
ffff3044:	d65f03c0 	ret

; usb_set_configuration(), sigle call from ffff3bb4
ffff3048:	aa1f03e0 	mov	x0, xzr

; =======================================================
; usb_reset_hw(), called from usb_init()

ffff3bb4:	97fffd25 	bl	0xffff3048				; usb_set_configuration()
ffff3bb8:	14000006 	b	0xffff3bd0				; return

ffff3bbc:	7100143f 	cmp	w1, #0x5
ffff3bc0:	54000061 	b.ne	0xffff3bcc			; we don't like it

; SRAM[132] & 0x60 == 0 && "request" == 5 is "SET ADDRESS"
ffff3bc4:	97fffcbc 	bl	0xffff2eb4				; usb_set_address()
ffff3bc8:	14000002 	b	0xffff3bd0				; return

; means "type" in not standard or "request" != (5, 6 or 9)
ffff3bcc:	97fffd14 	bl	0xffff301c				; usb_stall()

; done
ffff3bd0:	a9407bfd 	ldp	x29, x30, [sp]
ffff3bd4:	910043ff 	add	sp, sp, #0x10
ffff3bd8:	d65f03c0 	ret

; branch from code above when magic is not 0x472
; must be 0x471 but we check for it
ffff3bdc:	7111c43f 	cmp	w1, #0x471

ffff3bec:	39003001 	strb	w1, [x0, #12]

ffff3c0c:	39004002 	strb	w2, [x0, #16]

ffff3c1c:	39003802 	strb	w2, [x0, #14]

ffff3c24:	39003401 	strb	w1, [x0, #13]		; usb[12..19] = 0xff091000, app start address in SRAM
ffff3c28:	17ffffea 	b	0xffff3bd0				; return

; usb_try_run( payload ), called from otg_packet_rx()
;   x0 - src, pointer to "usb" memory
; checks the value in usb[4..5] for either 0x471 or 0x472 magic

; this routine will (if possible) run loaded code eventually

; A side note (this is in no way handled in this routine)
; 0x471 payloads will be in SRAM at offset 0x2000 (ff8c2000)
; 0x472 payloads will be in DDR ram at offset 0

; The value in SRAM + 12 determines whether USB is expected to be
; encrypted or not.  If it is 0, encryption is not required.
; in that case (with no encryption):

; 0x471 payload - this case is simple
;   -- reject it if it begins with RSAK
;   -- if it begins with RK32 launch it after the RK32.
;   -- otherwise, just launch it.

; 0x472 payload - this case is even simpler
;   -- just launch it at the start of the payload

; if the value in SRAM + 12 is non-zero, we are expecting encryption
; ** as near as I can tell, my chip is shipped without expecting
;   encryption, at least that is what I see looking at rkdeveloptool

; 0x471 payload -- 
;   -- check for RSAK
;   -- if present, call rump_roast() with payload
;   -- no RSAK: decrypt and try to run, skipping RK32

; 0x472 payload -- 
;   -- decrypt and run at start
ffff3c2c:	d10103ff 	sub	sp, sp, #0x40
ffff3c30:	a9037bfd 	stp	x29, x30, [sp, #48]
ffff3c34:	9100c3fd 	add	x29, sp, #0x30
ffff3c38:	a9025ff6 	stp	x22, x23, [sp, #32]
ffff3c3c:	a90157f4 	stp	x20, x21, [sp, #16]
ffff3c40:	f90007f3 	str	x19, [sp, #8]			; preserve x19

ffff3c44:	39402c01 	ldrb	w1, [x0, #11]
ffff3c48:	39402802 	ldrb	w2, [x0, #10]
ffff3c4c:	2a012041 	orr	w1, w2, w1, lsl #8
ffff3c50:	39402402 	ldrb	w2, [x0, #9]
ffff3c54:	39402003 	ldrb	w3, [x0, #8]
ffff3c58:	2a022062 	orr	w2, w3, w2, lsl #8
ffff3c5c:	2a014041 	orr	w1, w2, w1, lsl #16
ffff3c60:	51000834 	sub	w20, w1, #0x2			; w20 = usb[8..11] - 2
ffff3c64:	39404c02 	ldrb	w2, [x0, #19]
ffff3c68:	39404803 	ldrb	w3, [x0, #18]
ffff3c6c:	aa022062 	orr	x2, x3, x2, lsl #8
ffff3c70:	39404403 	ldrb	w3, [x0, #17]

ffff3c7c:	aa024062 	orr	x2, x3, x2, lsl #16
ffff3c80:	39403c03 	ldrb	w3, [x0, #15]

ffff3c88:	aa032083 	orr	x3, x4, x3, lsl #8
ffff3c8c:	39403404 	ldrb	w4, [x0, #13]
ffff3c90:	39403005 	ldrb	w5, [x0, #12]

ffff3c98:	aa034083 	orr	x3, x4, x3, lsl #16
ffff3c9c:	39401404 	ldrb	w4, [x0, #5]
ffff3ca0:	39401000 	ldrb	w0, [x0, #4]
ffff3ca4:	aa028075 	orr	x21, x3, x2, lsl #32	; x21 = usb[12..19]
ffff3ca8:	53007c36 	lsr	w22, w1, #0				; w22 = usb[8..11]
ffff3cac:	cb2142b3 	sub	x19, x21, w1, uxtw		; x19 = usb[12..19] - (usb[8..11] & 0xffffffff)

ffff3cb0:	2a042000 	orr	w0, w0, w4, lsl #8		; 16 bit value from usb[4..5]
ffff3cb4:	7111c41f 	cmp	w0, #0x471				; 0x471 magic value
ffff3cb8:	54000301 	b.ne	0xffff3d18			; branch if not 0x471
; ----------------------------------------------------------
; fall through with 0x471

; SRAM + 12 is set according to an efuse on startup
; a setting of zero indicates no encryption is required
ffff3cbc:	b0ff84e0 	adrp	x0, 0xff090000
ffff3cc0:	b9400c02 	ldr	w2, [x0, #12]

; encrypted or not, we look for RSAK
ffff3cc4:	b9400260 	ldr	w0, [x19]				; first word in payload
ffff3cc8:	b0ffffe1 	adrp	x1, 0xffff0000

ffff3cd0:	b9400021 	ldr	w1, [x1]
ffff3cd4:	34000522 	cbz	w2, 0xffff3d78			; if SRAM + 12 is zero, don't do this

ffff3cd8:	6b21401f 	cmp	w0, w1, uxtw			; does payload begin with "RSAK" ?
ffff3cdc:	54000541 	b.ne	0xffff3d84

; It does begin with RSAK

ffff3cf0:	52800021 	mov	w1, #0x1

ffff3d04:	b0ff84e0 	adrp	x0, 0xff090000
ffff3d08:	9100f000 	add	x0, x0, #0x3c

ffff3d14:	14000034 	b	0xffff3de4				; return

; here if not 0x471

ffff3d44:	34000500 	cbz	w0, 0xffff3de4			; return if 0

ffff3d48:	aa1403e0 	mov	x0, x20

ffff3d50:	aa1303e0 	mov	x0, x19					; payload
ffff3d54:	aa1403e1 	mov	x1, x20					; size
ffff3d58:	97fff39f 	bl	0xffff0bd4				; crypto2 ( x19, x20 )

ffff3d5c:	b0ff84e0 	adrp	x0, 0xff090000
ffff3d60:	9100f000 	add	x0, x0, #0x3c
ffff3d64:	91009000 	add	x0, x0, #0x24
ffff3d68:	97fff3c0 	bl	0xffff0c68				; crypto4 ( SRAM + 0x3c + 0x24 )

ffff3d6c:	340003a0 	cbz	w0, 0xffff3de0			; running at the first address in the image (0x472)

ffff3d70:	b9003abf 	str	wzr, [x21, #56]			; SRAM[56] = 0
ffff3d74:	1400001c 	b	0xffff3de4				; return

; ----------------------------------------------------------
; Branch here from from 0x471 code above when we see SRAM+12 zero (no encryption)
ffff3d78:	6b21401f 	cmp	w0, w1, uxtw			; check RSAK 
ffff3d7c:	54000340 	b.eq	0xffff3de4			; return if we have RSAK

; No RSAK, just run what we have
ffff3d80:	14000010 	b	0xffff3dc0

; ----------------------------------------------------
; Here for 0x471 when SRAM+12 is non-zero and payload does not begin with RSAK
; if the decryption works, we will  run it.

ffff3d9c:	aa1403e1 	mov	x1, x20

ffff3da4:	b0ff84e0 	adrp	x0, 0xff090000
ffff3da8:	9100f000 	add	x0, x0, #0x3c

ffff3db4:	b9003aff 	str	wzr, [x23, #56]			; SRAM[56] = 0
ffff3db8:	1400000b 	b	0xffff3de4				; return

; Now it is decrypted, so check the first 4 bytes.
; The following fetches from this:

ffff3dd0:	54000081 	b.ne	0xffff3de0			; GO run the payload anyway
ffff3dd4:	d2800080 	mov	x0, #0x4                   	// #4
ffff3dd8:	cb160000 	sub	x0, x0, x22

; We start running the payload at its start,
; possibly skipping an initial "RK33"

ffff3de0:	d63f0260 	blr	x19						; GO *************

; We could return from the code we run

; =======================================================
; usb_init()
; SRAM[160]+SRAM[164] is set as 64 bit ponter to SRAM[168], probaly "usb" related memory

ffff3e5c:	f94007f3 	ldr	x19, [sp, #8]			; restore x19

ffff3e68:	17fffd42 	b	0xffff3370				; enable interrupt and return

; verify ( payload, size ), called from otg_packet_rx()
; arguments:
;   x0 - src
;   x1 - size
; called from usb_try_run below
; return 1 if the checksum is invalid
; if the checksum is valid, "uncrypt" it and return 0
ffff3e6c:	d100c3ff 	sub	sp, sp, #0x30
ffff3e70:	a9027bfd 	stp	x29, x30, [sp, #32]
ffff3e74:	910083fd 	add	x29, sp, #0x20
ffff3e78:	a90157f4 	stp	x20, x21, [sp, #16]
ffff3e7c:	f90007f3 	str	x19, [sp, #8]				; _sp8 = x19, preserve x19

; get 2 byte object from the end of the buffer
; take care that it may not be nicely aligned
; put the object into w21

ffff3e88:	38744a60 	ldrb	w0, [x19, w20, uxtw]	; w0 = (u8)src[size-2]
ffff3e8c:	51000421 	sub	w1, w1, #0x1
ffff3e90:	38614a61 	ldrb	w1, [x19, w1, uxtw]		; w1 = (u8)src[size-1]
ffff3e94:	2a002035 	orr	w21, w1, w0, lsl #8			; w21 = size, 16 bits

; now w21 is the 2 bytes at the end of the payload.
; this is a checksum which we will now try to generate and match
; we exclude the checksum itself from the calculation
ffff3e98:	aa1303e0 	mov	x0, x19						; src
ffff3e9c:	aa1403e1 	mov	x1, x20						; size - 2
ffff3ea0:	9400001b 	bl	0xffff3f0c					; cksum ( src, size - 2 )
ffff3ea4:	aa0003e1 	mov	x1, x0
ffff3ea8:	52800020 	mov	w0, #0x1
ffff3eac:	6b2122bf 	cmp	w21, w1, uxth
ffff3eb0:	540000a1 	b.ne	0xffff3ec4				; return 1

; the above verifies our payload yields a proper checksum
; now decrypt it.
ffff3eb4:	aa1303e0 	mov	x0, x19
ffff3eb8:	aa1403e1 	mov	x1, x20
ffff3ebc:	94000029 	bl	0xffff3f60					uncrypt ( buf, count )
ffff3ec0:	2a1f03e0 	mov	w0, wzr						; return 0

; done
ffff3ec4:	f94007f3 	ldr	x19, [sp, #8]				; restore x19
ffff3ec8:	a94157f4 	ldp	x20, x21, [sp, #16]
ffff3ecc:	a9427bfd 	ldp	x29, x30, [sp, #32]
ffff3ed0:	9100c3ff 	add	sp, sp, #0x30
ffff3ed4:	d65f03c0 	ret

; =======================================================
; subroutine "usb_spin"

; The bootrom comes here once and forever if other methods
; of booting fail.

ffff3ed8:	d10043ff 	sub	sp, sp, #0x10

; note, usb communication is interrupt driven

; =======================================================
; One call to this,  enters right here
; cksum ( buf, n )
;
; looks to me like it generates some kind of checksum
ffff3f0c:	aa0003e2 	mov	x2, x0					; buf

ffff3f54:	91000442 	add	x2, x2, #0x1			; buf++
ffff3f58:	35fffe41 	cbnz	w1, 0xffff3f20		; loop
ffff3f5c:	d65f03c0 	ret

; =====================================================

; subroutine uncrypt ( buf, n )

; This is RC4 encryption.
; This ROM uses a precomputed block based on the following key
; unsigned char key[16]={124,78,3,4,85,5,9,7,45,44,123,56,23,13,23,17};
; Note that RC4 is the inverse of itself

; Called from several places
; -- no prolog
; w1 is a count (typically 0x200)
; x0 is some address

; This copies 256 bytes of stuff from this rom (at 0xffff46e0
; to SRAM at offset 0xf0
; I call SRAM + 0xf0 "code" in the below
;
; The copy is odd because we read 8 bytes, but only write 4 of those.
; So the image in ROM is twice as big as it ought to be.
; It really doesn't matter since we have plenty of extra
;  space in the ROM.  But it is odd.

; ==============================================================
; ==============================================================
; ==============================================================

; first core starting up comes here.  GO!
;  You might call this "main()"

; Startup branches here, it does NOT do a "bl"
;
; My notation here SRAM[x] refers to 32 bit objects in SRAM

; Some objects in SRAM (all 32 bit unless noted)
;
;  SRAM + 0  = cpu speed in Mhz (such as "24")
;  SRAM + 4  = mailbox to start cores 0xdeadbeaf
;  SRAM + 8  = mailbox to start cores -- address
;  SRAM + 12 = 0 or 1 (set 1 if fuse 0 is 0xff)
;  SRAM + 16 = boot-device identifier, used from subsequent loaders
;
;  SRAM + 12 governs whether this chip wants encrypted payloads
;   via USB or not.  If set, we expect encryption.
;  SRAM + 16 seems to only be set, never used
;    it is initially 0
;    it gets set to 10 if we boot from USB
;    it gets set to ??? in try_abc
;    from U-boot:
;       BROM_BOOTSOURCE_NAND = 1,
;       BROM_BOOTSOURCE_EMMC = 2,
;       BROM_BOOTSOURCE_SPINOR = 3,
;       BROM_BOOTSOURCE_SPINAND = 4,
;       BROM_BOOTSOURCE_SD = 5,
;       BROM_BOOTSOURCE_SPINOR_RK3588 = 6,
;       BROM_BOOTSOURCE_USB = 10,

; Here we have the usual subroutine prolog
ffff4288:	d10043ff 	sub	sp, sp, #0x10			; 16 bytes on stack
ffff428c:	a9007bfd 	stp	x29, x30, [sp]			; save x29 and x30 to stack
ffff4290:	910003fd 	mov	x29, sp					; get sp in x29

; post initial cpu clock speed of 24 Mhz
ffff4294:	52800300 	mov	w0, #0x18				; w = 24
ffff4298:	97fff103 	bl	0xffff06a4				; SRAM[0] = 24

ffff429c:	97fff58e 	bl	0xffff18d4				; CRU setup

; clear "encrypted USB required" flag
ffff42a0:	90ff84e0 	adrp	x0, 0xff090000		; SRAM
ffff42a4:	b9000c1f 	str	wzr, [x0, #12]			; SRAM[12] = 0
ffff42a8:	97fff084 	bl	0xffff04b8				; init_fuses()
ffff42ac:	97fff5a8 	bl	0xffff194c				; CRU, cpu clocks

; clear "XXX" flag
ffff42b0:	90ff84e0 	adrp	x0, 0xff090000
ffff42b4:	b900101f 	str	wzr, [x0, #16]			; SRAM[16] = 0

ffff42b8:	97fffaec 	bl	0xffff2e68				; try_four ()
ffff42bc:	97ffff07 	bl	0xffff3ed8				; usb_spin ()

ffff42c0:	14000000 	b	0xffff42c0				; just spin here forever

; ==============================================================

; subroutine, 3 calls  --  memory compare
; memcmp ( x0, x1, x2 ) -- more or less
; x0 - ptr1
; x1 - ptr2
; x2 - num

;  ==============================================

; subroutine, many calls --- memcpy
; just a wrapper around call to ffff4480
;  and the only call to that routine (so why not inline it?)
; x0 = destination address (usually in SRAM)

;  ==============================================
;
; subroutine, "memset"
;
; a typical call looks like this and never cares about
; a return value in x0.

;		mov	x2, #0x28
;		mov	x0, x20
;		mov	x1, xzr
;		bl	0xffff43c8
;
;	So x0 is some sort of address, x1 is always zero, x2 is a count
ffff43c8:	d10083ff 	sub	sp, sp, #0x20
ffff43cc:	f9000bfe 	str	x30, [sp, #16]

ffff43d4:	910023fd 	add	x29, sp, #0x8

; This is memset.
; we take the value in x1 as a byte and duplicate it 4 times in w2
; the count (in bytes) we do not change, but we do move
; the count and address to different registers
ffff43d8:	aa0003f3 	mov	x19, x0					; preserve x0 (addr)
ffff43dc:	2a0103e0 	mov	w0, w1					; x0 = val
ffff43e0:	aa0203e1 	mov	x1, x2					; x1 = count
ffff43e4:	12001c03 	and	w3, w0, #0xff			; w3 = val & 0xff
ffff43e8:	0b032063 	add	w3, w3, w3, lsl #8		; w3 = val << 8 + val
ffff43ec:	0b034062 	add	w2, w3, w3, lsl #16		; w2 = w3 << 16 + w3
ffff43f0:	aa1303e0 	mov	x0, x19					; restore x0 (addr)

; It is odd that this is the only call to a block of code below
; why not just put it inline right here?
; why jam "memcpy" between here and there?

; x0 is some address
; x1 is a count
; w2 is a value to store

; Accessory and "finish" to memcpy below.
